		Data: []byte(`package localfoo

import (
	"fmt"
)

func PrintSomethingElse() {
	fmt.Println("I am virtual filesystem printing else from localfoo/foo.go!")
}
`),

		t.Fatal(err)
	}

	_, err := i.EvalPath(`main.go`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
}

package interp_test

import (
	"bytes"
	"go/build"
	"go/parser"
	"go/token"

// The following tests sometimes (not always) crash with go1.21 but not with go1.20 or go1.22.
// The reason of failure is not obvious, maybe due to the runtime itself, and will be investigated separately.
// Also, the closure tests depend on an incompatible language change in go1.22, where `for` variables are now
// defined in body (thus reallocated at each loop). This is now the behavior in yaegi, so 1.21 produces
// different results.
var testsToSkipGo121 = map[string]bool{"cli6.go": true, "cli7.go": true, "issue-1276.go": true, "issue-1330.go": true, "struct11.go": true, "closure9.go": true, "closure10.go": true, "closure11.go": true, "closure12.go": true, "closure15.go": true, "closure16.go": true, "closure17.go": true, "closure18.go": true, "closure20.go": true, "for17.go": true, "for18.go": true, "for19.go": true}

var go121 = strings.HasPrefix(runtime.Version(), "go1.21")

func TestFile(t *testing.T) {
	filePath := "../_test/str.go"
	runCheck(t, filePath)

	t.Setenv("YAEGI_SPECIAL_STDIO", "1")

	baseDir := filepath.Join("..", "_test")
	files, err := os.ReadDir(baseDir)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}

	for _, file := range files {
		if filepath.Ext(file.Name()) != ".go" {
			continue
		}
		// Skip some tests which are problematic in go1.21 only.
		if go121 && testsToSkipGo121[file.Name()] {
			continue
		}
		file := file
		t.Run(file.Name(), func(t *testing.T) {
			runCheck(t, filepath.Join(baseDir, file.Name()))
		})
	}
}

func runCheck(t *testing.T, p string) {
	t.Helper()

	wanted, goPath, errWanted := wantedFromComment(p)
	if wanted == "" {
		t.Skip(p, "has no comment 'Output:' or 'Error:'")
	}
	wanted = strings.TrimSpace(wanted)

	if goPath == "" {
		goPath = build.Default.GOPATH
	}
	var stdout, stderr bytes.Buffer
	i := interp.New(interp.Options{GoPath: goPath, Stdout: &stdout, Stderr: &stderr})
	if err := i.Use(interp.Symbols); err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if err := i.Use(stdlib.Symbols); err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if err := i.Use(unsafe.Symbols); err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}

	_, err := i.EvalPath(p)
	if errWanted {
		if err == nil {
			t.Fatalf("got nil error, want: %q", wanted)
		}
		if res := strings.TrimSpace(err.Error()); !strings.Contains(res, wanted) {
			t.Errorf("got %q, want: %q", res, wanted)
		}
		return
	}

	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}

	res := strings.TrimSpace(stdout.String())
	// Remove path in output, to have results independent of location.
	res = strings.ReplaceAll(res, filepath.ToSlash(p)+":", "")
	if res != wanted {
		t.Errorf("\ngot:  %q,\nwant: %q", res, wanted)
	}
}

func wantedFromComment(p string) (res string, goPath string, err bool) {
	fset := token.NewFileSet()
	f, _ := parser.ParseFile(fset, p, nil, parser.ParseComments)
	if len(f.Comments) == 0 {
		return
	}
	text := f.Comments[len(f.Comments)-1].Text()
	if strings.HasPrefix(text, "GOPATH:") {
		parts := strings.SplitN(text, "\n", 2)
		text = parts[1]
		wd, err := os.Getwd()
		if err != nil {
			panic(err)
		}
		goPath = filepath.Join(wd, "..", "_test", strings.TrimPrefix(parts[0], "GOPATH:"))
	}
	if strings.HasPrefix(text, "Output:\n") {
		return strings.TrimPrefix(text, "Output:\n"), goPath, false
	}
	if strings.HasPrefix(text, "Error:\n") {
		return strings.TrimPrefix(text, "Error:\n"), goPath, true
	}
	return
}

STEP 31 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
Now let me understand the full AST structure and where `Doc` comments end up. For `//go:embed`, in Go's actual compiler, the directive comment must
immediately precede the `var` declaration, and can be followed by other comments. The `ast.GenDecl.Doc` field holds the comment group preceding the
declaration. Let's write a small Go program to inspect AST for embed directives, including grouped var form.

Let me also check how `ast.GenDecl` gets processed in ast.go - the `GenDecl` case just creates a varDecl node, then `ValueSpec` children are processed
via stack. Comments are not preserved in our node tree. So we need to collect embed info during AST conversion, before comments are dropped.

Plan:
1. Parse comments in all modes (add `parser.ParseComments` to parse() unconditionally, or at least for file parsing with inc false). Currently mode is
`parser.DeclarationErrors`; ParseComments only added when inc. For EvalPath and importSrc, inc is false. We need comments for embed directives. Add
ParseComments always.

2. In ast.go, when processing `*ast.GenDecl` for `token.VAR`, inspect `a.Doc` comments for `//go:embed` lines. Collect patterns and associate with the
varDecl node (store in a map keyed by node, or set on node).

3. After AST conversion (or in a later stage like cfg/gta), for each embedded var declaration, resolve the patterns against the source file's
directory using `interp.opt.filesystem` (the source filesystem). Produce content:
   - For string: file bytes as string.
   - For []byte: file bytes.
   - For embed.FS: a read-only fs.FS implementation.

   But at AST conversion time, we don't yet know the target type (that's determined in GTA/CFG). Actually we could resolve the file list first
(patterns -> set of files) and store the resolved file set on the node. Then at var init time, based on the type, construct the value. That's cleaner.

4. In genGlobalVarDecl, for each embedded global var node, replace its generation with a custom generator that sets the embedded value into the frame
slot (based on type: string, []byte, or embed.FS).

Now, for embed.FS: we need to implement a read-only fs.FS that satisfies fs.FS, fs.ReadFileFS, fs.ReadDirFS, and whose opened directories implement
fs.ReadDirFile. We need `ReadDir` entries sorted by name. `ReadFile` returns independent copy each call. `Open` on a file returns fs.File that
implements fs.ReadDirFile? No — only opened directories implement fs.ReadDirFile. Files just implement fs.File (Read, Stat, Close). Actually fs.File
requires Stat, Read, Close. Directories need ReadDirFile: ReadDir, Stat, Read, Close.

We can implement this from scratch, or wrap `fstest.MapFS`? But `fstest.MapFS` is in testing package, not available in production use. We must
implement our own FS type.

Let me design:
- `embedFS` struct holds a map[string][]byte (files) and directory entries sorted, plus maybe the underlying source fs to read on demand. Since
`ReadFile` must return independent copy, easiest is to read from source fs at Open/ReadFile time. But fs.FS operations like ReadDir need to list
directories. Since the source fs (realFS or MapFS) can ReadDir, we could delegate. But easier: at embed resolution time, walk the matched files and
build a full in-memory map of all files in the tree. Then embedFS is just a map-based FS.

Let me think about what patterns mean. Go embed semantics:
- Pattern matching uses `path.Match` (slash-separated).
- A pattern like `*.txt` matches files in the source directory? Actually in real Go embed, patterns are relative to package dir and can include `/`.
- A pattern matching a directory embeds entire tree.
- Files starting with `.` or `_` excluded unless `all:` prefix.
- Patterns matching no files produce an error.

For the filesystem, `fs.ReadDir` returns DirEntry with `IsDir()`. We can walk recursively.

Let me check how `fs.WalkDir` works and whether the source FS supports it. realFS is os.Open passthrough; fs.WalkDir uses ReadDir. That works on real
OS. MapFS supports it too.

So embed resolution:
1. Given patterns and baseDir (dir of source file):
   - For each pattern, use `path.Match(pattern, name)` where name is relative path from baseDir. But real Go embed doesn't allow `..` or absolute
patterns. We'll enforce that patterns cannot start with `/`, contain `..`, etc. (Go has restrictions; `path.Match` may match `.` and `..`, but we
should reject).
   - Actually Go's embed matches patterns against paths relative to the package directory. For pattern without slash, it matches top-level files AND
directories. Let me implement: list all entries recursively under baseDir, compute relative slash paths, match each path with the pattern. But a
pattern like `dir` should match directory `dir` and embed whole tree. A pattern like `dir/*` matches all entries in dir (files and subdirectories).
Pattern `*.txt` matches files at top level only because `path.Match` `*` doesn't match `/`. Good.
   - However, `path.Match` would also match directories. If a matched path is a directory, include entire tree.
   - Exclusions: files (and dirs?) whose base name starts with `.` or `_` are excluded unless `all:` prefix. In Go, "If a pattern names a directory,
all files in the subtree rooted at that directory are embedded (recursively), except that files with names beginning with . or _ are excluded." The
exclusion applies to files. Also `all:` prefix includes files beginning with . or _. It seems the `all:` prefix affects the pattern: `all:dir`
includes hidden files in dir. The Go docs: "If a pattern begins with the prefix all:, then the walker matches all files, including files whose names
begin with . or _". So `all:` applies to that pattern's tree.

2. Collect matched files. If a matched path is a directory, walk recursively and include all files (subject to exclusions).

3. For string/[]byte: require exactly one file matched. If pattern matched directory with tree, and only one file in tree? Actually in Go,
string/[]byte with directory pattern requires exactly one file in the tree. Let's implement: collect all matched file paths; if exactly one, read it;
else error "patterns must resolve to exactly one file".

4. For embed.FS: one or more files. Build a map of relative paths (within the embedded root) to bytes. In Go, embed.FS path names: for a pattern
`file.txt`, the file is at path `file.txt`; for `dir` it includes `dir/sub/file.txt`. So keys are relative paths including the matched directory
prefix. For pattern `*.txt` matching `a.txt`, key is `a.txt`.

Now, `embed.FS` type: we need it to be recognized by the interpreter's type system as `embed.FS`. If scripts import `embed` and use `embed.FS`, the
binary symbol will be the Go `embed.FS` type. The variable declared as `var fs embed.FS` gets a reflect.Type of the binary embed.FS. We need to set
the reflect.Value to our custom FS value, which must be assignable to `embed.FS`.

This is the tricky part. `embed.FS` is a struct type in the Go standard library with unexported fields. Our custom implementation cannot be assigned
to `embed.FS` reflect.Type because `embed.FS` is a concrete struct with unexported fields that we can't construct directly. Let me check what
`embed.FS` looks like in Go source.

// Copyright 2020 The Go Authors. All rights reserved.
// Use of this source code is governed by a BSD-style
// license that can be found in the LICENSE file.

// Package embed provides access to files embedded in the running Go program.
//
// Go source files that import "embed" can use the //go:embed directive
// to initialize a variable of type string, []byte, or [FS] with the contents of
// files read from the package directory or subdirectories at compile time.
//
// For example, here are three ways to embed a file named hello.txt
// and then print its contents at run time.
//
// Embedding one file into a string:
//
//	import _ "embed"
//
//	//go:embed hello.txt
//	var s string
//	print(s)
//
// Embedding one file into a slice of bytes:
//
//	import _ "embed"
//
//	//go:embed hello.txt
//	var b []byte
//	print(string(b))
//
// Embedded one or more files into a file system:
//
//	import "embed"
//
//	//go:embed hello.txt
//	var f embed.FS
//	data, _ := f.ReadFile("hello.txt")
//	print(string(data))
//
// # Directives
//
// A //go:embed directive above a variable declaration specifies which files to embed,
// using one or more path.Match patterns.
//
// The directive must immediately precede a line containing the declaration of a single variable.
// Only blank lines and ‘//’ line comments are permitted between the directive and the declaration.
//
// The type of the variable must be a string type, or a slice of a byte type,
// or [FS] (or an alias of [FS]).
//
// For example:
//
//	package server
//
//	import "embed"
//
//	// content holds our static web server content.
//	//go:embed image/* template/*
//	//go:embed html/index.html
//	var content embed.FS
//
// The Go build system will recognize the directives and arrange for the declared variable
// (in the example above, content) to be populated with the matching files from the file system.
//
// The //go:embed directive accepts multiple space-separated patterns for
// brevity, but it can also be repeated, to avoid very long lines when there are
// many patterns. The patterns are interpreted relative to the package directory
// containing the source file. The path separator is a forward slash, even on
// Windows systems. Patterns may not contain ‘.’ or ‘..’ or empty path elements,
// nor may they begin or end with a slash. To match everything in the current
// directory, use ‘*’ instead of ‘.’. To allow for naming files with spaces in
// their names, patterns can be written as Go double-quoted or back-quoted
// string literals.
//
// If a pattern names a directory, all files in the subtree rooted at that directory are
// embedded (recursively), except that files with names beginning with ‘.’ or ‘_’
// are excluded. So the variable in the above example is almost equivalent to:
//
//	// content is our static web server content.
//	//go:embed image template html/index.html
//	var content embed.FS
//
// The difference is that ‘image/*’ embeds ‘image/.tempfile’ while ‘image’ does not.
// Neither embeds ‘image/dir/.tempfile’.
//
// If a pattern begins with the prefix ‘all:’, then the rule for walking directories is changed
// to include those files beginning with ‘.’ or ‘_’. For example, ‘all:image’ embeds
// both ‘image/.tempfile’ and ‘image/dir/.tempfile’.
//
// The //go:embed directive can be used with both exported and unexported variables,
// depending on whether the package wants to make the data available to other packages.
// It can only be used with variables at package scope, not with local variables.
//
// Patterns must not match files outside the package's module, such as ‘.git/*’, symbolic links,
// 'vendor/', or any directories containing go.mod (these are separate modules).
// Patterns must not match files whose names include the special punctuation characters  " * < > ? ` ' | / \ and :.
// Matches for empty directories are ignored. After that, each pattern in a //go:embed line
// must match at least one file or non-empty directory.
//
// If any patterns are invalid or have invalid matches, the build will fail.
//

//
// # Strings and Bytes
//
// The //go:embed line for a variable of type string or []byte can have only a single pattern,
// and that pattern can match only a single file. The string or []byte is initialized with
// the contents of that file.
//
// The //go:embed directive requires importing "embed", even when using a string or []byte.
// In source files that don't refer to [embed.FS], use a blank import (import _ "embed").
//
// # File Systems
//
// For embedding a single file, a variable of type string or []byte is often best.
// The [FS] type enables embedding a tree of files, such as a directory of static
// web server content, as in the example above.
//
// FS implements the [io/fs] package's [FS] interface, so it can be used with any package that
// understands file systems, including [net/http], [text/template], and [html/template].
//
// For example, given the content variable in the example above, we can write:
//
//	http.Handle("/static/", http.StripPrefix("/static/", http.FileServer(http.FS(content))))
//
//	template.ParseFS(content, "*.tmpl")
//
// # Tools
//
// To support tools that analyze Go packages, the patterns found in //go:embed lines
// are available in “go list” output. See the EmbedPatterns, TestEmbedPatterns,
// and XTestEmbedPatterns fields in the “go help list” output.
package embed

import (
	"errors"
	"internal/bytealg"
	"internal/stringslite"
	"io"
	"io/fs"
	"time"
)

// An FS is a read-only collection of files, usually initialized with a //go:embed directive.
// When declared without a //go:embed directive, an FS is an empty file system.
//
// An FS is a read-only value, so it is safe to use from multiple goroutines
// simultaneously and also safe to assign values of type FS to each other.
//
// FS implements fs.FS, so it can be used with any package that understands
// file system interfaces, including net/http, text/template, and html/template.
//
// See the package documentation for more details about initializing an FS.
type FS struct {
	// The compiler knows the layout of this struct.
	// See cmd/compile/internal/staticdata's WriteEmbed.
	//
	// The files list is sorted by name but not by simple string comparison.
	// Instead, each file's name takes the form "dir/elem" or "dir/elem/".
	// The optional trailing slash indicates that the file is itself a directory.
	// The files list is sorted first by dir (if dir is missing, it is taken to be ".")
	// and then by base, so this list of files:
	//
	//	p
	//	q/
	//	q/r
	//	q/s/
	//	q/s/t
	//	q/s/u
	//	q/v
	//	w
	//
	// is actually sorted as:
	//
	//	p       # dir=.    elem=p
	//	q/      # dir=.    elem=q
	//	w       # dir=.    elem=w
	//	q/r     # dir=q    elem=r
	//	q/s/    # dir=q    elem=s
	//	q/v     # dir=q    elem=v
	//	q/s/t   # dir=q/s  elem=t
	//	q/s/u   # dir=q/s  elem=u
	//
	// This order brings directory contents together in contiguous sections
	// of the list, allowing a directory read to use binary search to find
	// the relevant sequence of entries.
	files *[]file
}

// split splits the name into dir and elem as described in the
// comment in the FS struct above. isDir reports whether the
// final trailing slash was present, indicating that name is a directory.
func split(name string) (dir, elem string, isDir bool) {
	name, isDir = stringslite.CutSuffix(name, "/")
	i := bytealg.LastIndexByteString(name, '/')
	if i < 0 {
		return ".", name, isDir
	}
	return name[:i], name[i+1:], isDir
}

var (
	_ fs.ReadDirFS  = FS{}
	_ fs.ReadFileFS = FS{}
)

// A file is a single file in the FS.
// It implements fs.FileInfo and fs.DirEntry.
type file struct {
	// The compiler knows the layout of this struct.
	// See cmd/compile/internal/staticdata's WriteEmbed.
	name string
	data string
	hash [16]byte // truncated SHA256 hash
}

var (
	_ fs.FileInfo = (*file)(nil)
	_ fs.DirEntry = (*file)(nil)
)

func (f *file) Name() string               { _, elem, _ := split(f.name); return elem }
func (f *file) Size() int64                { return int64(len(f.data)) }
==== embed.go fs methods ====
	files := *f.files
	i := sortSearch(len(files), func(i int) bool {
		idir, ielem, _ := split(files[i].name)
		return idir > dir || idir == dir && ielem >= elem
	})
	if i < len(files) && stringslite.TrimSuffix(files[i].name, "/") == name {
		return &files[i]
	}
	return nil
}

// readDir returns the list of files corresponding to the directory dir.
func (f FS) readDir(dir string) []file {
	if f.files == nil {
		return nil
	}
	// Binary search to find where dir starts and ends in the list
	// and then return that slice of the list.
	files := *f.files
	i := sortSearch(len(files), func(i int) bool {
		idir, _, _ := split(files[i].name)
		return idir >= dir
	})
	j := sortSearch(len(files), func(j int) bool {
		jdir, _, _ := split(files[j].name)
		return jdir > dir
	})
	return files[i:j]
}

// Open opens the named file for reading and returns it as an [fs.File].
//
// The returned file implements [io.Seeker] and [io.ReaderAt] when the file is not a directory.
func (f FS) Open(name string) (fs.File, error) {
	file := f.lookup(name)
	if file == nil {
		return nil, &fs.PathError{Op: "open", Path: name, Err: fs.ErrNotExist}
	}
	if file.IsDir() {
		return &openDir{file, f.readDir(name), 0}, nil
	}
	return &openFile{file, 0}, nil
}

// ReadDir reads and returns the entire named directory.
func (f FS) ReadDir(name string) ([]fs.DirEntry, error) {
	file, err := f.Open(name)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	dir, ok := file.(*openDir)
	if !ok {
		return nil, &fs.PathError{Op: "read", Path: name, Err: errors.New("not a directory")}
	}
	list := make([]fs.DirEntry, len(dir.files))
	for i := range list {
		list[i] = &dir.files[i]
	}
	return list, nil
}

// ReadFile reads and returns the content of the named file.
func (f FS) ReadFile(name string) ([]byte, error) {
	file, err := f.Open(name)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	ofile, ok := file.(*openFile)
	if !ok {
		return nil, &fs.PathError{Op: "read", Path: name, Err: errors.New("is a directory")}
	}
	return []byte(ofile.f.data), nil
}

// An openFile is a regular file open for reading.
type openFile struct {
	f      *file // the file itself
	offset int64 // current read offset
}

var (
	_ io.Seeker   = (*openFile)(nil)
	_ io.ReaderAt = (*openFile)(nil)
)

func (f *openFile) Close() error               { return nil }
func (f *openFile) Stat() (fs.FileInfo, error) { return f.f, nil }

func (f *openFile) Read(b []byte) (int, error) {
	if f.offset >= int64(len(f.f.data)) {
		return 0, io.EOF
	}
	if f.offset < 0 {
		return 0, &fs.PathError{Op: "read", Path: f.f.name, Err: fs.ErrInvalid}
	}
	n := copy(b, f.f.data[f.offset:])
	f.offset += int64(n)
	return n, nil
}

func (f *openFile) Seek(offset int64, whence int) (int64, error) {
	switch whence {
	case 0:
		// offset += 0
	case 1:
		offset += f.offset
	case 2:
		offset += int64(len(f.f.data))
	}
	if offset < 0 || offset > int64(len(f.f.data)) {
		return 0, &fs.PathError{Op: "seek", Path: f.f.name, Err: fs.ErrInvalid}
	}
	f.offset = offset
	return offset, nil
}

func (f *openFile) ReadAt(b []byte, offset int64) (int, error) {
	if offset < 0 || offset > int64(len(f.f.data)) {
		return 0, &fs.PathError{Op: "read", Path: f.f.name, Err: fs.ErrInvalid}
	}
	n := copy(b, f.f.data[offset:])

There's no embed package in stdlib. That means if the user does `import "embed"` and uses `embed.FS`, the interpreter would fail because the package
isn't available. Unless... we need to add `embed` to stdlib exports so `embed.FS` is a known type. But we can't easily construct an actual `embed.FS`
value with content due to unexported fields.

Hmm. Let's think about what the feature request really asks. It says target types include `embed.FS`. Since this is an interpreter, perhaps we can
implement a custom type that the interpreter treats as `embed.FS`. But the script needs to import "embed" and declare `var f embed.FS`.

Option A: Add an `embed` package to stdlib that exports a custom type (our own implementation) named `FS`, and register it with the interpreter. Then
scripts using `embed.FS` would get our custom type. But the script would expect `embed.FS` to be the standard library type; if we provide our own type
with same name, it should work within the interpreted program because all uses of `embed.FS` resolve to our exported type.

But wait — the binary `embed` package is a real package in the Go runtime. If a script embeds binary symbols from `embed`, it should ideally get the
real `embed.FS`. But since we can't construct it, we need a custom implementation.

Actually, maybe we can construct an `embed.FS` using reflection by setting the unexported `files` field. `reflect.Value.Set` on unexported fields
panics unless using `unsafe`. But there's `reflect.NewAt` with unsafe tricks. However, the `file` type is also unexported in embed package, with
fields `name`, `data string`, `hash [16]byte`. We could use unsafe to construct values... but that's fragile and depends on Go internals. Not good.

Better approach: Provide our own `embed` package in stdlib with a custom FS implementation. But then it's not the real embed.FS. Is that acceptable?
The requirement says "embed.FS -- one or more files as a read-only filesystem. Implements fs.FS, fs.ReadFileFS, and fs.ReadDirFS." It doesn't say it
must be the actual stdlib `embed.FS` type. So a custom type named `embed.FS` that implements those interfaces would satisfy the feature.

{{.License}}

{{if .BuildTags}}// +build {{.BuildTags}}{{end}}

package {{.Dest}}

import (
{{- range $key, $value := .Imports }}
	{{- if $value}}
	"{{$key}}"
	{{- end}}
{{- end}}
{{- if or .Val .Typ }}
	"{{.ImportPath}}"
{{- end}}
	"reflect"
)

func init() {
	Symbols["{{.PkgName}}"] = map[string]reflect.Value{
		{{- if .Val}}
		// function, constant and variable definitions
		{{range $key, $value := .Val -}}
			{{- if $value.Addr -}}
				"{{$key}}": reflect.ValueOf(&{{$value.Name}}).Elem(),
			{{else -}}
				"{{$key}}": reflect.ValueOf({{$value.Name}}),
			{{end -}}
		{{end}}

		{{- end}}
		{{- if .Typ}}
		// type definitions
		{{range $key, $value := .Typ -}}
			"{{$key}}": reflect.ValueOf((*{{$value}})(nil)),
		{{end}}

		{{- end}}
		{{- if .Wrap}}
		// interface wrapper definitions
		{{range $key, $value := .Wrap -}}
			"_{{$key}}": reflect.ValueOf((*{{$value.Name}})(nil)),
		{{end}}
		{{- end}}
	}
}
{{range $key, $value := .Wrap -}}
	// {{$value.Name}} is an interface wrapper for {{$key}} type
	type {{$value.Name}} struct {
		IValue interface{}
		{{range $m := $value.Method -}}
		W{{$m.Name}} func{{$m.Param}} {{$m.Result}}
		{{end}}
	}
	{{range $m := $value.Method -}}
		func (W {{$value.Name}}) {{$m.Name}}{{$m.Param}} {{$m.Result}} {
			{{- if eq $m.Name "String"}}
			if W.WString == nil {
				return ""
			}
			{{end}}
			{{- $m.Ret}} W.W{{$m.Name}}{{$m.Arg -}}
		}
	{{end}}
{{end}}
`

// Val stores the value name and addressable status of symbols.
type Val struct {
	Name string // "package.name"
	Addr bool   // true if symbol is a Var
}

// Method stores information for generating interface wrapper method.
type Method struct {
	Name, Param, Result, Arg, Ret string
}

// Wrap stores information for generating interface wrapper.
type Wrap struct {
	Name   string
	Method []Method
}

	"logFatalln":    true,

REASONING_CONTENT
Interesting - `embed` is excluded from stdlib's generated packages (the comment says "go list std | grep -v internal | grep -v '.' | grep -v unsafe |
grep -v syscall" but embed has no exported symbols except FS type, so the extract tool probably produced nothing? Actually let's try running extract
on embed to see what it generates. But since embed.FS has no exported methods that produce values, the extract might generate only the FS type. Let me
check if we can add it.

Actually let's think about the interpreter's approach to a custom `embed.FS`. Since `embed.FS` is a concrete type with unexported fields, if we add
stdlib's embed wrapper, it would export `FS: reflect.ValueOf((*embed.FS)(nil))`. The interpreter would know the type `embed.FS` (a binary type). But
we can't set its content.

Alternative: We could implement `embed.FS` support entirely at the interpreter level with a custom type that the interpreter recognizes. When the
script declares `var f embed.FS`, the type is resolved via binary package `embed`. We could detect this during CFG and instead use our own custom
reflect type. But that's very invasive.

Better alternative: Implement a custom package `embed` in stdlib that provides our own `FS` type (implemented by us, with exported fields maybe or
unexported but constructed via our own code). Since it's our own package, we can make `FS` a struct with a `files map[string][]byte` field, and define
methods Open, ReadDir, ReadFile etc. Then in interpreted code `var f embed.FS` gets our custom type, and `f.ReadFile(...)` works.

But wait: if the script does `import "embed"` and also uses `embed.FS` in a way that requires it to be the real embed.FS (e.g., passed to `fs.FS`
interface, or `http.FileServer(http.FS(f))`), our custom type implementing `fs.FS` works.

However, there's a subtlety: the interpreted code may compare `embed.FS{}` or use `var _ fs.FS = embed.FS{}` (compile-time assertion). Our custom type
can satisfy that too.

So plan:
- Create `stdlib/embed/embed.go` (or a new package) that defines a custom `FS` type and registers it in the stdlib Symbols map under the key
`"embed"`.
- Add `embed` to the stdlib generation list? Actually we can hand-write the wrapper rather than generating. Let's look at how stdlib packages are
structured and whether they can be in subdirectories. The generated files are in stdlib package directory with build tags. We can add a new file
`stdlib/embed.go` in package stdlib that registers the custom embed package symbols.

But there's a complication: `stdlib.Symbols` is a single map. Each generated file does `Symbols["embed"] = map[string]reflect.Value{...}` in init().
We can add our own init that sets `Symbols["embed"]`. But the `embed` package would also need to be importable by the extract tool? No, we just
hand-write it.

Actually, we could just define our custom FS type directly in a stdlib file. But the type must be accessible to interpreted code as `embed.FS`. We
need:
```go
Symbols["embed"] = map[string]reflect.Value{
    "FS": reflect.ValueOf((*FS)(nil)),
}
```
where `FS` is our custom type. But `embed` package's name in the map key should be "embed". Let me check how the interpreter resolves `import "embed"`
to stdlib symbols. The `Use(stdlib.Symbols)` loads all packages. When script imports "embed", it looks up Symbols["embed"] probably.

Let me verify how import resolution works for binary packages. Search in cfg.go or interp.go for binPkg and package lookup.

// getPrompt returns a function which prints a prompt only if input is a terminal.
func getPrompt(in io.Reader, out io.Writer) func(reflect.Value) {
	forcePrompt, _ := strconv.ParseBool(os.Getenv("YAEGI_PROMPT"))
	if forcePrompt {
		return doPrompt(out)
	}
	s, ok := in.(interface{ Stat() (os.FileInfo, error) })
	if !ok {
		return func(reflect.Value) {}
	}
	stat, err := s.Stat()
	if err == nil && stat.Mode()&os.ModeCharDevice != 0 {
		return doPrompt(out)
	}
	return func(reflect.Value) {}
}

// Use loads binary runtime symbols in the interpreter context so
// they can be used in interpreted code.
func (interp *Interpreter) Use(values Exports) error {
	for k, v := range values {
		importPath := path.Dir(k)
		packageName := path.Base(k)

		if k == "." && v["MapTypes"].IsValid() {
			// Use mapping for special interface wrappers.
			for kk, vv := range v["MapTypes"].Interface().(map[reflect.Value][]reflect.Type) {
				interp.mapTypes[kk] = vv
			}
			continue
		}

		if importPath == "." {
			return fmt.Errorf("export path %[1]q is missing a package name; did you mean '%[1]s/%[1]s'?", k)
		}

		if importPath == selfPrefix {
			interp.hooks.Parse(v)
			continue
		}

		if interp.binPkg[importPath] == nil {
			interp.binPkg[importPath] = make(map[string]reflect.Value)
			interp.pkgNames[importPath] = packageName
		}

		for s, sym := range v {
			interp.binPkg[importPath][s] = sym
		}
		if k == selfPath {
			interp.binPkg[importPath]["Self"] = reflect.ValueOf(interp)
		}
	}

	// Checks if input values correspond to stdlib packages by looking for one
	// well known stdlib package path.
	if _, ok := values["fmt/fmt"]; ok {
		fixStdlib(interp)

		// Load stdlib generic source.
		for _, s := range gen.Sources {
			if _, err := interp.Compile(s); err != nil {
				return err
			}
		}
	}
	return nil
}

// fixStdlib redefines interpreter stdlib symbols to use the standard input,
// output and errror assigned to the interpreter. The changes are limited to
// the interpreter only.
// Note that it is possible to escape the virtualized stdio by
// read/write directly to file descriptors 0, 1, 2.
func fixStdlib(interp *Interpreter) {

Now, we need to be careful: the interpreter's type system has special handling for binary types. `reflect.TypeOf((*FS)(nil)).Elem()` would be the
custom FS type. The var's frame slot would be of that type. Our embed generator can construct a value of that type using our own constructor.

So we'll define in a new stdlib subpackage (e.g., `stdlib/embed`) our custom FS, and register it. But note: the stdlib package itself is a single
package with many generated files, all `package stdlib`. We can add a file `stdlib/embed.go` in package stdlib that defines `FS` and registers symbols
in init(). However, the generated files are named `go1_21_embed.go` etc. We'll add a file that's not generated, e.g. `stdlib/embed.go` with build tag
go1.21 (or no build tag, but go.mod says go 1.21, so fine).

imports `embed`; we need the symbol map. The cleanest is to add to stdlib.

But there's a subtlety: the interpreter's `Use` checks `if k == selfPath` and `fixStdlib`. It iterates `for k, v := range values` where values is
stdlib.Symbols. Our entry `"embed/embed"` -> map{"FS": ...} will be added. Good.

// Copyright 2023 The Go Authors. All rights reserved.

// Package cmp provides types and functions related to comparing
// ordered values.
package cmp

// Ordered is a constraint that permits any ordered type: any type
// that supports the operators < <= >= >.
// If future releases of Go add new ordered types,
// this constraint will be modified to include them.
//
// Note that floating-point types may contain NaN ("not-a-number") values.
// An operator such as == or < will always report false when
// comparing a NaN value with any other value, NaN or not.
// See the [Compare] function for a consistent way to compare NaN values.
type Ordered interface {
	~int | ~int8 | ~int16 | ~int32 | ~int64 |
		~uint | ~uint8 | ~uint16 | ~uint32 | ~uint64 | ~uintptr |
		~float32 | ~float64 |
		~string
}

// Less reports whether x is less than y.
// For floating-point types, a NaN is considered less than any non-NaN,
// and -0.0 is not less than (is equal to) 0.0.
func Less[T Ordered](x, y T) bool {
	return (isNaN(x) && !isNaN(y)) || x < y
}

// Compare returns
//
//	-1 if x is less than y,
//	 0 if x equals y,
//	+1 if x is greater than y.
//
// For floating-point types, a NaN is considered less than any non-NaN,
// a NaN is considered equal to a NaN, and -0.0 is equal to 0.0.
func Compare[T Ordered](x, y T) int {
---
// Copyright 2021 The Go Authors. All rights reserved.
// Use of this source code is governed by a BSD-style
// license that can be found in the LICENSE file.

// Package maps defines various functions useful with maps of any type.
package maps

// Equal reports whether two maps contain the same key/value pairs.
// Values are compared using ==.
func Equal[M1, M2 ~map[K]V, K, V comparable](m1 M1, m2 M2) bool {
	if len(m1) != len(m2) {
		return false
	}
	for k, v1 := range m1 {
		if v2, ok := m2[k]; !ok || v1 != v2 {
			return false
		}
	}
	return true
}

					}

			case ident == "init":
				// init functions do not get declared as per the Go spec.
			default:
				asImportName := path.Join(ident, baseName)
				if _, exists := sc.sym[asImportName]; exists {
					// redeclaration error
					err = n.cfgErrorf("%s redeclared in this block", ident)
					return false
				}
				// Add a function symbol in the package name space except for init
				sc.sym[ident] = &symbol{kind: funcSym, typ: n.typ, node: n, index: -1}
			}
			if !n.typ.isComplete() && !genericMethod {
				revisit = append(revisit, n)
			}
			return false

		case importSpec:
			var name, ipath string
			if len(n.child) == 2 {
				ipath = constToString(n.child[1].rval)
				name = n.child[0].ident
			} else {
				ipath = constToString(n.child[0].rval)
			}
			// Try to import a binary package first, or a source package
			var pkgName string
			if packageName := path.Base(ipath); path.Dir(ipath) == packageName {
				ipath = packageName
			}
			if pkg := interp.binPkg[ipath]; pkg != nil {
				switch name {
				case "_": // no import of symbols
				case ".": // import symbols in current scope
					for n, v := range pkg {
						typ := v.Type()
						kind := binSym
						if isBinType(v) {
							typ = typ.Elem()
							kind = typeSym
						}
						sc.sym[n] = &symbol{kind: kind, typ: valueTOf(typ, withScope(sc)), rval: v}
					}
				default: // import symbols in package namespace
					if name == "" {
						name = interp.pkgNames[ipath]
					}

					// If an incomplete type exists, delete it
					if sym, exists := sc.sym[name]; exists && sym.kind == typeSym && sym.typ.incomplete {
						delete(sc.sym, name)
					}

					// Imports of a same package are all mapped in the same scope, so we cannot just
					// map them by their names, otherwise we could have collisions from same-name
					// imports in different source files of the same package. Therefore, we suffix
					// the key with the basename of the source file.
					name = path.Join(name, baseName)
					if sym, exists := sc.sym[name]; !exists {
						sc.sym[name] = &symbol{kind: pkgSym, typ: &itype{cat: binPkgT, path: ipath, scope: sc}}
						break
					} else if sym.kind == pkgSym && sym.typ.cat == srcPkgT && sym.typ.path == ipath {
						// ignore re-import of identical package
						break
					}

					// redeclaration error. Not caught by the parser.
					err = n.cfgErrorf("%s redeclared in this block", name)
					return false
				// TODO(mpl): move any of that code to typecheck?
				c.typ.node = c
				if !c.typ.assignableTo(typ) {
					err = c.cfgErrorf("cannot use %v (type %v) as type %v in return argument", c.ident, c.typ.cat, typ.cat)
					return
				}
				if c.typ.cat == nilT {
					// nil: Set node value to zero of return type
					c.rval = reflect.New(typ.TypeOf()).Elem()
				}
			}

		case selectorExpr:
			wireChild(n)
			n.typ = n.child[0].typ
			n.recv = n.child[0].recv
			if n.typ == nil {
				err = n.cfgErrorf("undefined type")
				break
			}
			switch {
			case n.typ.cat == binPkgT:

				name := n.child[1].ident
				pkg := n.child[0].sym.typ.path
				if s, ok := interp.binPkg[pkg][name]; ok {
					if isBinType(s) {
						n.typ = valueTOf(s.Type().Elem())
					} else {
						n.typ = valueTOf(fixPossibleConstType(s.Type()), withUntyped(isValueUntyped(s)))
						n.rval = s

							n.sym = &symbol{kind: bltnSym, node: n, rval: s}
							n.ident = pkg + "." + name
						}
					}
					n.action = aGetSym
					n.gen = nop
				} else {
					err = n.cfgErrorf("package %s \"%s\" has no symbol %s", n.child[0].ident, pkg, name)
				}

REASONING_CONTENT
OK so import `embed` resolves to binPkg["embed"], and `embed.FS` selector resolves to the type `FS` via `isBinType(s)` (pointer-to-type). Good.

Now let me think about the overall design more concretely.

## Design

### 1. Add `parser.ParseComments` to parse()

Currently:
```go
mode := parser.DeclarationErrors
...
if inc {
    ...
    mode |= parser.ParseComments
}
```
Change to always include ParseComments:
```go
mode := parser.DeclarationErrors | parser.ParseComments
```
or keep the inc logic but also set it when not inc. We can just always add.

### 2. Capture embed directives during AST conversion

In ast.go's `case *ast.GenDecl:` for `token.VAR`, we have access to `a.Doc` (comment group). We need to extract `//go:embed` lines and store them. The
`GenDecl` node is created via `addChild(&root, anc, pos, kind, aNop)`. We can store embed patterns on the node. The node struct has a `meta
interface{}` field we can use.

But wait: multiple embed lines may precede one var declaration, and the Doc field of GenDecl contains all comments immediately preceding the decl.
Actually `ast.GenDecl.Doc` contains the comment group immediately before the declaration. But there's a subtlety: comments like `// content holds...`
and `//go:embed image/* template/*` and `//go:embed html/index.html` all are in one comment group? Let me check. In Go, consecutive `//` comments with
no blank line form a single CommentGroup. So `Doc.Text()` would contain all lines. We can split into lines and pick out `//go:embed` lines.

But we also need to handle grouped `var ( ... )` form:
```go
//go:embed hello.txt
var (
    s string
    //go:embed other.txt
    t string
)
```
In grouped form, each `//go:embed` precedes a `ValueSpec` within the GenDecl, and `ValueSpec` has its own `Doc` field (comment group). So we need to
capture:
- `GenDecl.Doc` for directives before `var` or before `var (` (applies to first ValueSpec)
- `ValueSpec.Doc` for directives before individual specs in grouped form.

Actually in Go, for a single var declaration:
```go
//go:embed hello.txt
var s string
```
the comment is in `GenDecl.Doc`.

For grouped:
```go
var (
    //go:embed a.txt
    s string
    //go:embed b.txt
    t string
)
```
comments are in `ValueSpec.Doc`.

Also there's a form:
```go
//go:embed a.txt
var (
    s string
)
```
comment is in GenDecl.Doc, applies to first (and only) ValueSpec.

So during ast.go processing of ValueSpec, we need to know whether it has embed directives from its own Doc or inherited from GenDecl.Doc.

Approach: In `case *ast.GenDecl` for VAR, create the varDecl node, parse `a.Doc` for embed lines, and store them in a map from varDecl node ->
patterns, or set on the varDecl node's meta. Then when processing ValueSpec children, if the ValueSpec has its own Doc with embed lines, store those
on the ValueSpec node; otherwise, if the varDecl has embed patterns and this is the first ValueSpec, inherit them. But ast.go uses a stack; the
ValueSpec's anc is the varDecl. We can check `anc.node` when processing ValueSpec.

Actually, simpler: We can do the embed pattern collection separately after parsing, using the Go AST directly before converting to yaegi nodes. In
`parse()`, we return `f *ast.File`. We could scan the AST for GenDecl/ValueSpec with embed comments and store in a map keyed by position or by node
index. But then ast.go would need to correlate.

Alternative cleaner approach: Modify `ast()` to, when it creates the varDecl node, extract embed directives from both GenDecl.Doc and each
ValueSpec.Doc. Since ast.go's `case *ast.ValueSpec` has access to `a` (the ValueSpec) and `anc.node` (the varDecl node), we can:
- In GenDecl case: set a field on the varDecl node `meta = embedPatternsFromComments(a.Doc)` (only for VAR).
- In ValueSpec case: if `a.Doc` has embed patterns, set them on the valueSpec node meta. Else if the anc varDecl has patterns and this is the first
ValueSpec child, copy them to the valueSpec node meta. Also clear the varDecl's patterns after use so subsequent ValueSpecs don't inherit.

But wait, in grouped form, `var ( //go:embed a.txt\n s string )` - the GenDecl.Doc patterns should apply to the first ValueSpec. But also `//go:embed
a.txt` directly above `var (`? Yes.

Actually, hold on: is the comment before `var (` part of GenDecl.Doc? Let me verify with a quick Go program. Let me write a scratch program to inspect
the AST.

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"go/ast"
	"go/parser"
	"go/token"
)

func main() {
	src := `package main

import _ "embed"

//go:embed hello.txt
var s string

//go:embed a.txt
//go:embed b.txt
var (
	x string
	//go:embed c.txt
	y string
)

// note comment
//go:embed z.txt
var z string
`
	fset := token.NewFileSet()
	f, err := parser.ParseFile(fset, "x.go", src, parser.ParseComments)
	if err != nil { panic(err) }
	for _, d := range f.Decls {
		if gd, ok := d.(*ast.GenDecl); ok && gd.Tok == token.VAR {
			fmt.Printf("GenDecl Doc: %q\n", gd.Doc.Text())
			for _, s := range gd.Specs {
				vs := s.(*ast.ValueSpec)
				fmt.Printf("  ValueSpec %v Doc: %q\n", vs.Names[0].Name, vs.Doc.Text())
			}
		}
	}
}
EOF

import (

func main() {
	src := `package main

//go:embed hello.txt
var s string

//go:embed b.txt
var (
	x string
	//go:embed c.txt
	y string
)

// note comment
//go:embed z.txt
var z string
`
	fset := token.NewFileSet()
	f, _ := parser.ParseFile(fset, "x.go", src, parser.ParseComments)
	for _, d := range f.Decls {
		gd, ok := d.(*ast.GenDecl)
		if !ok || gd.Tok != token.VAR { continue }
		fmt.Println("GenDecl:")
		if gd.Doc != nil { printCG("  Doc", gd.Doc) }
		for _, s := range gd.Specs {
			vs := s.(*ast.ValueSpec)
			fmt.Printf("  ValueSpec %v:\n", vs.Names[0].Name)
			if vs.Doc != nil { printCG("    Doc", vs.Doc) }
		}
	}
}

func printCG(label string, cg *ast.CommentGroup) {
	fmt.Printf("%s: Text=%q\n", label, cg.Text())
	for _, c := range cg.List {
		fmt.Printf("    %q at %v\n", c.Text, c.Slash)
	}
}

func (interp *Interpreter) ast(f ast.Node) (string, *node, error) {
	var err error
	var root *node
	var anc astNode
	var st nodestack
	pkgName := "main"

	addChild := func(root **node, anc astNode, pos token.Pos, kind nkind, act action) *node {
		var i interface{}
		nindex := atomic.AddInt64(&interp.nindex, 1)
		n := &node{anc: anc.node, interp: interp, index: nindex, pos: pos, kind: kind, action: act, val: &i, gen: builtin[act]}
		n.start = n
		if anc.node == nil {
			*root = n
		} else {
			anc.node.child = append(anc.node.child, n)
			if anc.node.action == aCase {
				ancAst := anc.ast.(*ast.CaseClause)
				if len(ancAst.List)+len(ancAst.Body) == len(anc.node.child) {
					// All case clause children are collected.
					// Split children in condition and body nodes to desambiguify the AST.
					nindex = atomic.AddInt64(&interp.nindex, 1)
					body := &node{anc: anc.node, interp: interp, index: nindex, pos: pos, kind: caseBody, action: aNop, val: &i, gen: nop}

					if ts := anc.node.anc.anc; ts.kind == typeSwitch && ts.child[1].action == aAssign {
						// In type switch clause, if a switch guard is assigned, duplicate the switch guard symbol
						// in each clause body, so a different guard type can be set in each clause
						name := ts.child[1].child[0].ident
						nindex = atomic.AddInt64(&interp.nindex, 1)
						gn := &node{anc: body, interp: interp, ident: name, index: nindex, pos: pos, kind: identExpr, action: aNop, val: &i, gen: nop}
						body.child = append(body.child, gn)
					}

					// Add regular body children
					body.child = append(body.child, anc.node.child[len(ancAst.List):]...)
					for i := range body.child {
						body.child[i].anc = body
					}
					anc.node.child = append(anc.node.child[:len(ancAst.List)], body)
				}
			}
		}
		return n
	}

	// Populate our own private AST from Go parser AST.
	// A stack of ancestor nodes is used to keep track of current ancestor for each depth level
	ast.Inspect(f, func(nod ast.Node) bool {
		anc = st.top()
		var pos token.Pos
		if nod != nil {
			pos = nod.Pos()
		}
		switch a := nod.(type) {
		case nil:
			anc = st.pop()

		case *ast.ArrayType:
			st.push(addChild(&root, anc, pos, arrayType, aNop), nod)

		case *ast.AssignStmt:
			var act action
			var kind nkind
			if len(a.Lhs) > 1 && len(a.Rhs) == 1 {
				if a.Tok == token.DEFINE {
					kind = defineXStmt
				} else {
					kind = assignXStmt
				}
				act = aAssignX
			} else {
				kind = assignStmt
				switch a.Tok {
				case token.ASSIGN:
					act = aAssign
				case token.ADD_ASSIGN:
					act = aAddAssign
				case token.AND_ASSIGN:
					act = aAndAssign
				case token.AND_NOT_ASSIGN:
					act = aAndNotAssign
				case token.DEFINE:
					kind = defineStmt
					act = aAssign
				case token.SHL_ASSIGN:
					act = aShlAssign
				case token.SHR_ASSIGN:
					act = aShrAssign
				case token.MUL_ASSIGN:
					act = aMulAssign
				case token.OR_ASSIGN:
					act = aOrAssign
				case token.QUO_ASSIGN:
					act = aQuoAssign
				case token.REM_ASSIGN:
					act = aRemAssign
				case token.SUB_ASSIGN:
					act = aSubAssign
				case token.XOR_ASSIGN:
					act = aXorAssign
				}
			}
			n := addChild(&root, anc, pos, kind, act)
			n.nleft = len(a.Lhs)
			n.nright = len(a.Rhs)
			st.push(n, nod)

		case *ast.BasicLit:
			n := addChild(&root, anc, pos, basicLit, aNop)
			n.ident = a.Value

Now, let's design the embed resolution function.

### Embed pattern resolution

```go
func (interp *Interpreter) resolveEmbed(dir string, patterns []string) (*embedData, error)
```

Where embedData contains:
```go
type embedData struct {
    files map[string][]byte // path -> content, paths relative to dir
    names []string          // sorted relative paths
}
```

Algorithm:
1. For each pattern:
   - Validate: not absolute (no leading "/"), no "." or ".." path elements, not empty, not ending with "/", and `path.Match` must not error. Also
reject backslash.
   - Strip `all:` prefix -> all bool, pat := pattern[4:].
   - Use `fs.Glob`? No, `fs.Glob` only returns names matching pattern at top level, and doesn't recurse into directories. We need custom walk.

2. To find matches: We need to list the directory tree. We can recursively walk `dir` using fs.ReadDir, collecting all entries (dirs and files) with
relative paths. But `fs.ReadDir` returns entries for one dir at a time. Then for each relative path (file or dir), test `path.Match(pat, relPath)`. If
a dir matches, we recursively include all files under it (with exclusions). If a file matches, include it.

But there's an important Go embed nuance: a pattern like `dir` matches the directory `dir` and embeds its tree. A pattern like `*.txt` matches only
top-level files. Our path.Match on relPath handles that since `*` doesn't match `/`.

What about patterns with `/` like `image/*`? path.Match matches `image/a.png`, and also `image/subdir` (directory). If `image/subdir` matches, include
its tree.

Also, pattern `image/*` matches files directly in image, including `image/dir` dir -> include tree of image/dir. Good.

But what about pattern `image` matching dir `image` -> include tree. Good.

Now, collecting the tree: For each matched directory path, recursively walk and include all descendant files. We should avoid double-including the
same file (dedupe). In Go, if multiple patterns match same file, it's not an error; it just includes it once.

Exclusions: files (and directories?) starting with `.` or `_` are excluded unless `all:` prefix. Go docs: "files with names beginning with . or _ are
excluded". Does this apply to directories too? In practice, `image/*` doesn't embed `image/.hidden` but does it include files under
`image/.hiddendir`? The doc says `image/*` embeds `image/.tempfile` while `image` does not. Neither embeds `image/dir/.tempfile`. So the exclusion is
per-file-name during walk: any file (and directory) whose base name starts with . or _ is skipped, so a hidden directory's contents are also skipped
because you can't descend into it. For `all:`, hidden files and dirs are included.

So the walk should apply exclusion to each entry name at every level (unless all).

Let me define a recursive walker:

```go
func walkEmbed(fsys fs.FS, root, rel string, all bool, include func(rel string, isDir bool) error) error {
    entries, err := fs.ReadDir(fsys, root)
    if err != nil { return err }
    for _, e := range entries {
        name := e.Name()
        if !all && (strings.HasPrefix(name, ".") || strings.HasPrefix(name, "_")) {
            continue
        }
        childRel := path.Join(rel, name)  // if rel=="" childRel=name
        childRoot := path.Join(root, name)
        if e.IsDir() {
            // recurse
            walkEmbed(fsys, childRoot, childRel, all, include)
        } else {
            include(childRel, false)
        }
    }
}
```

But for pattern matching we need to walk the whole tree first to find matches, then for matched dirs walk again. Simpler: do a full recursive walk
collecting all paths (files and dirs) into a list. Then for each pattern, for each candidate path, test match. For dirs that match, include all files
under that dir (from the collected list). For files that match, include them.

Actually even simpler: collect all entries (relative paths) of both files and dirs in a single full walk (respecting exclusions based on `all`?
No—exclusion depends on pattern's `all:` flag, so we need per-pattern behavior). Let's collect all entries with their exclusions computed per-pattern.
Or collect ALL entries (no exclusion), and during matching apply exclusion based on whether any path component starts with . or _ (unless all). That's
cleaner.

Let me collect all files and dirs:
```go
type fsEntry struct {
    path string
    isDir bool
}
```
Walk the whole dir recursively, no exclusion. For each entry, determine "hidden" = any path component (before last) starts with . or _? Actually the
rule: files with names beginning with . or _ are excluded. For a file `dir/.tempfile`, the file's name starts with . → excluded. For
`dir/.hiddendir/file`, the directory name starts with . → we don't descend, so `file` isn't embedded. So the effective rule: an entry is visible if
all its path components' base names do not start with . or _. (For the top-level pattern matching, the pattern is matched against the full relative
path; if a dir component is hidden, then files under it shouldn't be included.)

So for each candidate entry, `visible := !anyComponentHidden(path)` unless `all:`. Then:
- If path.Match(pat, entry.path) and entry is dir → include all files under this dir (all files whose path has this dir as prefix) that are visible
(under all rules). Since if a component under the dir is hidden, those files are excluded.
- If path.Match(pat, entry.path) and entry is file → include if visible.

But there's a subtlety: `path.Match(pat, dirpath)` where dirpath is `image/.hidden` — if pattern is `all:image/*`, hidden is true but all means
visible. If pattern is `image/*`, hidden entries excluded.

Also, for pattern `image/*`, path.Match would match `image/.tempfile` (a file). We exclude it because visible false. Good.

Now, there's another subtlety: matching a file under a matched dir. If pattern `image` matches dir `image`, we include all visible files under image.
If pattern `image/*` also matches file `image/a.txt`, it's included too (dedupe).

Dedupe: use a map[string]bool.

Validation of patterns:
- `path.Match(pat, "x")` will return error for invalid patterns. We can call it with a dummy to validate.
- Reject patterns with empty elements, "." or ".." elements, leading/trailing "/", absolute paths, backslash. Also reject patterns matching "." (the
root) — Go doesn't allow. But not strictly necessary; let's implement basic checks per Go embed rules.

Now, pattern parsing from comment lines: Each `//go:embed` line's content after the directive is `patterns`. E.g. `//go:embed image/* template/*` →
patterns ["image/*", "template/*"]. Multiple lines combine.

We need to extract from `cg.List[i].Text` which is like `//go:embed hello.txt`. Trim the `//` and whitespace, check prefix "go:embed". Then take rest.

Also there can be quoted patterns: `//go:embed "hello world.txt"`. The Go spec allows patterns written as Go string literals. The feature request says
"Each directive line contains space-separated glob patterns (path.Match syntax)." It doesn't mention quoted patterns, but to be robust, we could
handle quoted. Let me handle basic unquoted; maybe support quoted too for robustness. Actually the task says "space-separated glob patterns
(path.Match syntax)" — I'll implement basic space-separated and also handle quoted strings to be safe. Hmm, but implementing quoted parsing adds
complexity. Let me consider: yaegi tests likely won't use quoted patterns. I'll implement space-separated split and also support quoted via
strconv.Unquote if a token is quoted. Actually, let's keep it simple and robust: parse using a small tokenizer that handles double-quoted and
back-quoted strings, since Go allows it and it's not too hard.

Wait, but if a pattern contains a space and is unquoted, Go would error. Our split would just produce multiple patterns. Let's implement a tokenizer:
- iterate over line
- skip spaces
- if token starts with `"` or `` ` ``, read until matching quote, unquote via strconv.Unquote
- else read until space.
This handles both.

### Where to resolve

We need to resolve patterns at compile time, and store the result so genGlobalVars can use it. Since files could be modified between Compile and
Execute (or between multiple Execute calls), resolving at compile time is fine and matches Go semantics (embedded at compile time).

I'll resolve in ast.go during AST conversion, and store `*embedData` on the ValueSpec node's `meta`. That way genGlobalVars can access it.

### How to inject values in genGlobalVars

`genGlobalVarDecl` takes `nodes []*node` (each a valueSpec or defineStmt for a global var). For each node n:
- if n.meta is *embedData (embedded), set `n.gen = genEmbedVar` where genEmbedVar sets the frame slot based on n.typ.
- For a valueSpec, normally `n.gen = reset`. We override with our own generator.

But wait: `reset` is set in cfg's valueSpec case. We can override in genGlobalVarDecl before wireChild/genRun. Since genGlobalVarDecl is called after
cfg and before run, and genRun is called... Actually in Execute, genRun(p.root) is called first, but global varDecls aren't wired at that point, so
their exec isn't generated yet. Then genGlobalVarDecl wires them, and run(varNode) calls setExec which calls n.gen. So overriding n.gen in
genGlobalVarDecl works.

But there's a subtlety: `genGlobalVarDecl` may be called multiple times (once per Execute). And `n.gen` might already be set to reset. We override
each time.

Let me write `genEmbedVar`:

```go
func genEmbedVar(n *node) {
    next := getExec(n.tnext)
    data := n.meta.(*embedData)
    // single child (the ident)
    typ := n.child[0].typ
    // or n.typ for valueSpec
    ...
    n.exec = func(f *frame) bltn {
        // set f.data[index] based on type
        return next
    }
}
```

For valueSpec, the children are [ident, type]. The frame index is `n.child[0].findex`, and frame type is `n.child[0].typ.frameType()`. For string:
`f.data[idx] = reflect.ValueOf(content).Convert(frameType)`? Actually frame data elements are reflect.Values of the variable's type. We can set with
`reflect.ValueOf(content).Convert(typ.TypeOf())` if types match. For string type, `reflect.ValueOf(stringContent)` is of type string; if the declared
type is `string` (binary or source), it should be assignable. If it's a custom string alias? Go embed allows string types (named string types).
`reflect.ValueOf(...).Convert(frameType)` should work for named string types if the underlying is string. For []byte, similarly.

For embed.FS: our custom FS type. We'll create a value using a constructor `newEmbedFS(files map[string][]byte) FS` (returns our custom FS value),
then convert to the frame type. Since the frame type is our custom FS type (if we registered our custom FS), `reflect.ValueOf(fsValue)` should have
exactly that type.

But wait: what if the script's `embed.FS` resolves to the real binary embed.FS (if we had stdlib generated for embed)? We're not generating it, so
it'll be our custom type. Good.

However, there's a subtlety with how `valueSpec` type resolution works. Let me examine how `n.typ` is set for valueSpec. In cfg case valueSpec:
```go
n.gen = reset
l := len(n.child) - 1
if n.typ = n.child[l].typ; n.typ == nil {
    if n.typ, err = nodeType(interp, sc, n.child[l]); err != nil { return }
}
for _, c := range n.child[:l] {
    ...
    c.typ = n.typ
    c.findex = index
}
```
So `n.typ` is the type node, and child ident gets typ. For an embed FS var, `n.typ` is an `*itype` representing `embed.FS`. Its `frameType()` returns
the reflect.Type.

Now, `genEmbedVar` should set `f.data[index]` for each ident child (usually one). In valueSpec, there's usually one ident; but `var a, b string` with
no initializer is possible, and embed directives can only apply to a single variable. Go requires the declaration to be a single variable (the
directive must immediately precede a line containing the declaration of a single variable). So we should error if there are multiple names. We'll
handle that: if embedData and len(children) != 1 → error.

Actually, for valueSpec with embed, children are [ident, type] → len = 2, nleft = 1. `len(n.child)-1` = 1.

Now, for the `defineStmt` case (var with initializer), embed directives are not allowed in Go (can't have both embed and initializer). We should error
if someone writes `//go:embed x.txt` + `var s = "init"`. Let's add validation: if embedData is set and the node kind is not valueSpec (i.e., it has an
initializer), return error.

Let me now design the EmbedFS type in interp:

```go
// EmbedFS is a read-only filesystem populated by //go:embed directives.
type EmbedFS struct {
    files map[string][]byte // relative file path -> content
}
```

Methods:
- `Open(name string) (fs.File, error)`
- `ReadFile(name string) ([]byte, error)`
- `ReadDir(name string) ([]fs.DirEntry, error)`

`Open`:
- validate name is clean, no leading "/".
- if name refers to a directory (there exists at least one file path with prefix name+"/"), return an `embedDir` that implements `fs.ReadDirFile`.
- if name is a file, return `embedFile` with content; independent copy? The requirement says `ReadFile` returns an independent copy each call. Our
`ReadFile` will copy bytes each call. `Open` returns a file that reads from the stored []byte — but if we store the slice and the user mutates via
Read into their buffer, that's fine; the underlying data isn't exposed directly. But to be safe, Open can also use a copy.

Actually simpler: store content as []byte. For Open file, return `&embedFile{data: content, offset: 0}` where data is a fresh copy of content. For
ReadFile, return `[]byte` copy.

`embedFile` implements fs.File: `Stat() (fs.FileInfo, error)`, `Read([]byte) (int, error)`, `Close() error`. Also io.Seeker and io.ReaderAt maybe for
compatibility (the real embed.FS files implement Seeker and ReaderAt). The requirement doesn't mention, but let's implement Seeker for completeness.
Actually fs.File only requires Stat, Read, Close. We'll implement those plus maybe Seek.

`embedDir` implements fs.ReadDirFile: `ReadDir(n int) ([]fs.DirEntry, error)`, `Stat`, `Read` (returns error), `Close`.

DirEntry: use a simple struct implementing fs.DirEntry: Name, IsDir, Type, Info.

We need to know which directories exist. We can compute from the file map: a directory path exists if there's at least one file with that dir as
prefix. Also the root "." exists if map non-empty. Also intermediate dirs.

For `ReadDir(name)`, list entries (files and subdirs) directly under name, sorted by name. Entries are fs.DirEntry with Type() = 0 for files,
fs.ModeDir for dirs.

Let me implement helper functions:
```go
func (f *EmbedFS) hasDir(name string) bool
func (f *EmbedFS) entries(name string) []fs.DirEntry
```

Normalize name: strip trailing "/", clean. "." means root.

### How genEmbedVar constructs the EmbedFS value

Since the type is `interp.EmbedFS` (our custom type), we can do:
```go
efs := &EmbedFS{files: data.files}
v := reflect.ValueOf(*efs)  // type EmbedFS
```
But `reflect.ValueOf(*efs)` gives a value of type EmbedFS (not pointer). The frame slot type is EmbedFS (since script declares `var f embed.FS` and
our exported type is a value type `EmbedFS`). So `v` should be assignable. Then `f.data[index].Set(v)` or
`f.data[index].Set(reflect.ValueOf(*efs).Convert(frameType))`.

But wait: `reflect.ValueOf(*efs)` is not addressable but Set is fine.

Now, an important subtlety: The `itype` for `embed.FS` is `valueTOf(reflect.TypeOf(EmbedFS{}))`. Let me verify how binary type selector works. In cfg
selectorExpr case: `n.typ = valueTOf(s.Type().Elem())` where s = reflect.ValueOf((*EmbedFS)(nil)) so s.Type() = *EmbedFS, s.Type().Elem() = EmbedFS.
So `n.typ` has cat valueT and TypeOf() = EmbedFS. frameType() returns EmbedFS. Good.

### How to know in genEmbedVar whether type is string, []byte, or FS

We can compare:
```go
rt := n.typ.TypeOf() // for valueSpec, n.typ is the type itype
```
- If rt.Kind() == reflect.String → string.
- If rt.Kind() == reflect.Slice && rt.Elem().Kind() == reflect.Uint8 → []byte. But need to allow named byte slice types? Go allows "slice of a byte
type". So `type MyBytes []byte` should work. rt.Elem().Kind() == reflect.Uint8 handles []byte and named byte? Actually named byte type's Kind() is
Uint8 too. Good.
- If rt == reflect.TypeOf(EmbedFS{}) → FS.
- Else error.

But there's another approach: use the itype's identity. Let's use reflect kind checks.

For string: content is a string; if rt is a named string type, `reflect.ValueOf(content).Convert(rt)` works.

For []byte: content []byte; convert to rt.

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"testing"
	"testing/fstest"

	"github.com/traefik/yaegi/interp"
	"github.com/traefik/yaegi/stdlib"
)

func TestEmbedRepro(t *testing.T) {
	fsys := fstest.MapFS{
		"main.go": &fstest.MapFile{
			Data: []byte(`package main

import (
	_ "embed"
	"fmt"
)

//go:embed hello.txt
var s string

func main() {
	fmt.Println(s)
}
`),
		},
		"hello.txt": &fstest.MapFile{Data: []byte("hello world")},
	}
	i := interp.New(interp.Options{SourcecodeFilesystem: fsys})
	if err := i.Use(stdlib.Symbols); err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	_, err := i.EvalPath("main.go")
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("EvalPath error: %v", err)
	}
}
EOF

	}
}

```go
package interp

import (
        "errors"
        "io"
        "io/fs"
        "path"
        "sort"
        "strings"
        "time"
)

// EmbedFS is a read-only in-memory filesystem built from //go:embed directives.
// It implements fs.FS, fs.ReadFileFS and fs.ReadDirFS.
type EmbedFS struct {
        files map[string][]byte
}

var _ fs.FS = (*EmbedFS)(nil)
var _ fs.ReadFileFS = (*EmbedFS)(nil)
var _ fs.ReadDirFS = (*EmbedFS)(nil)

// Open opens the named file or directory.
func (f *EmbedFS) Open(name string) (fs.File, error) {
        name = path.Clean("/" + name)
        ...
}
```

Wait, for fs.FS, name is slash-separated relative to root, and must not start with "/". We'll handle by cleaning.

Let me define internal helpers:

```go
func (f *EmbedFS) clean(name string) (string, error) {
        if !fs.ValidPath(name) {
                return "", &fs.PathError{Op: "open", Path: name, Err: fs.ErrInvalid}
        }
        return path.Clean(name), nil
}
```
`fs.ValidPath` checks no leading slash, no empty elements, no "." or ".." etc. Actually fs.ValidPath allows "."? It says "ValidPath reports whether
the given path name is valid for use as a path in a file system". It rejects ".", "..", empty. So root "." is not valid for Open. But ReadDir(".")
should work for fs.ReadDirFS? The fs.ReadDirFS interface: "ReadDir reads the named directory". Typically "." is allowed for root. Hmm, fs.FS.Open(".")
is usually valid? `os.DirFS.Open(".")` works. `fs.ValidPath` returns false for "."? Let me check. Actually fs.ValidPath(".") returns false. But
embed.FS's Open(".", "")? Real embed.FS: Open(".") returns the root directory. Let me check. In embed.FS, `lookup` handles dir "." specially. So we
should allow "." for directories.

Let me handle: if name == "." or name == "", treat as root dir.

Implementation:

```go
func (f *EmbedFS) lookup(name string) (isDir bool, ok bool) {
        if f.files == nil {
                return false, false
        }
        name = strings.TrimSuffix(name, "/")
        if name == "." {
                return true, len(f.files) > 0
        }
        if _, ok := f.files[name]; ok {
                return false, true
        }
        // check directory: exists if some file path has prefix name + "/"
        prefix := name + "/"
        for p := range f.files {
                if strings.HasPrefix(p, prefix) {
                        return true, true
                }
        }
        return false, false
}
```

Open:
```go
func (f *EmbedFS) Open(name string) (fs.File, error) {
        if name == "" {
                name = "."
        }
        if !fs.ValidPath(name) && name != "." {
                return nil, &fs.PathError{Op:"open", Path:name, Err:fs.ErrInvalid}
        }
        name = path.Clean(name)
        isDir, ok := f.lookup(name)
        if !ok {
                return nil, &fs.PathError{Op:"open", Path:name, Err:fs.ErrNotExist}
        }
        if isDir {
                return &embedDir{fs: f, name: name, entries: f.readDir(name)}, nil
        }
        return &embedFile{name: name, data: append([]byte(nil), f.files[name]...)}, nil
}
```

ReadFile:
```go
func (f *EmbedFS) ReadFile(name string) ([]byte, error) {
        if !fs.ValidPath(name) {
                return nil, &fs.PathError{Op:"read", Path:name, Err:fs.ErrInvalid}
        }
        name = path.Clean(name)
        isDir, ok := f.lookup(name)
        if !ok {
                return nil, &fs.PathError{Op:"read", Path:name, Err:fs.ErrNotExist}
        }
        if isDir {
                return nil, &fs.PathError{Op:"read", Path:name, Err:errors.New("is a directory")}
        }
        return append([]byte(nil), f.files[name]...), nil
}
```

ReadDir:
```go
func (f *EmbedFS) ReadDir(name string) ([]fs.DirEntry, error) {
        if name == "" { name = "." }
        if !fs.ValidPath(name) && name != "." { ... }
        name = path.Clean(name)
        isDir, ok := f.lookup(name)
        if !ok { ... }
        if !isDir {
                return nil, &fs.PathError{Op:"read", Path:name, Err:errors.New("not a directory")}
        }
        return f.readDir(name), nil
}

func (f *EmbedFS) readDir(name string) []fs.DirEntry {
        prefix := ""
        if name != "." { prefix = name + "/" }
        set := map[string]fs.DirEntry{}
        for p := range f.files {
                if !strings.HasPrefix(p, prefix) { continue }
                rest := p[len(prefix):]
                if rest == "" { continue }
                elem, sub, _ := strings.Cut(rest, "/")
                full := prefix + elem
                if _, ok := set[full]; ok { continue }
                if sub != "" {
                        set[full] = &embedDirEntry{name: elem, isDir: true}
                } else {
                        set[full] = &embedDirEntry{name: elem, isDir: false}
                }
        }
        names := make([]string, 0, len(set))
        for k := range set { names = append(names, k) }
        sort.Strings(names)
        entries := make([]fs.DirEntry, len(names))
        for i, n := range names {
                entries[i] = set[n]
                // adjust name to basename for DirEntry.Name()
                if e := entries[i].(*embedDirEntry); e != nil {
                        e.name = path.Base(n)
                }
        }
        return entries
}
```

Hmm, simpler: store entries keyed by basename but dedupe. Let me just collect set of names (basename) and sort. Since each entry name is the last
element.

```go
func (f *EmbedFS) readDir(name string) []fs.DirEntry {
        prefix := ""
        if name != "." {
                prefix = name + "/"
        }
        type entry struct {
                basename string
                isDir    bool
        }
        m := map[string]entry{}
        for p := range f.files {
                if !strings.HasPrefix(p, prefix) {
                        continue
                }
                rest := p[len(prefix):]
                if rest == "" {
                        continue
                }
                base, sub, _ := strings.Cut(rest, "/")
                e, ok := m[base]
                if !ok {
                        e = entry{basename: base}
                }
                if sub != "" {
                        e.isDir = true
                }
                m[base] = e
        }
        names := make([]string, 0, len(m))
        for base := range m {
                names = append(names, base)
        }
        sort.Strings(names)
        entries := make([]fs.DirEntry, len(names))
        for i, base := range names {
                e := m[base]
                entries[i] = &embedDirEntry{name: e.basename, isDir: e.isDir}
        }
        return entries
}
```

embedDirEntry:
```go
type embedDirEntry struct {
        name  string
        isDir bool
}

func (e *embedDirEntry) Name() string { return e.name }
func (e *embedDirEntry) IsDir() bool { return e.isDir }
func (e *embedDirEntry) Type() fs.FileMode {
        if e.isDir { return fs.ModeDir }
        return 0
}
func (e *embedDirEntry) Info() (fs.FileInfo, error) {
        return &embedFileInfo{name: e.name, isDir: e.isDir}, nil
}

type embedFileInfo struct {
        name string
        isDir bool
}

func (i *embedFileInfo) Size() int64 { return 0 } // not used for dirs; for files we need size
func (i *embedFileInfo) Mode() fs.FileMode { if i.isDir {return fs.ModeDir}; return 0 }

func (i *embedFileInfo) IsDir() bool { return i.isDir }
func (i *embedFileInfo) Sys() interface{} { return nil }
```
For files we need Size. Let's have embedFileInfo carry size. Simpler: for file entries we can construct an info with size. Actually DirEntry.Info()
returns FileInfo; let's make it store size.

embedFile:
```go
type embedFile struct {
        name string
        data []byte
        offset int64
}
func (f *embedFile) Close() error { return nil }
func (f *embedFile) Read(p []byte) (int, error) {
        if f.offset >= int64(len(f.data)) { return 0, io.EOF }
        n := copy(p, f.data[f.offset:])
        f.offset += int64(n)
        return n, nil
}
func (f *embedFile) Stat() (fs.FileInfo, error) {
        return &embedFileInfo{name: f.name, size: int64(len(f.data))}, nil
}
func (f *embedFile) Seek(offset int64, whence int) (int64, error) { ... }
```

embedDir:
```go
type embedDir struct {
        name string
        entries []fs.DirEntry
        offset int
}
func (d *embedDir) Close() error { return nil }
func (d *embedDir) Read(p []byte) (int, error) { return 0, &fs.PathError{Op:"read", Path:d.name, Err:errors.New("is a directory")} }
func (d *embedDir) Stat() (fs.FileInfo, error) { return &embedFileInfo{name:d.name, isDir:true}, nil }
func (d *embedDir) ReadDir(n int) ([]fs.DirEntry, error) {
        if n <= 0 {
                d.offset = 0
                res := make([]fs.DirEntry, len(d.entries))
                copy(res, d.entries)
                return res, nil
        }
        if d.offset >= len(d.entries) { return nil, io.EOF }
        res := d.entries[d.offset:]
        if n < len(res) { res = res[:n] }
        d.offset += len(res)
        return res, nil
}
```

Also, for fs.DirEntry.Name, it returns base name (not full path). Good.

Now, the `readDir` function returns sorted by name. Requirement satisfied.
